# Changelog

## Unreleased

- **The Around town box, less of it** (players' finding, 2026-11-07: "How do I disable the window in the top right corner? I couldn't find it anywhere in the settings menu"; settings version 306).
  - It can be turned off: `[Output] NewsTo = off`, now also on the settings panel's UI page as "The Around town box, top right (corner; ticker; game feed; off)", where it had shown only as "NewsTo".
  - It's up less. The town's regard of you keeps the box up for 30 seconds after you come into a town, or when it changes, not the whole time you're there (`[Output] AroundTownStaysSeconds`; 0 brings back always). Each item stays a little shorter (5.5 seconds and a bit for its length, at most 12, where it was 7 and at most 16), and at most four wait their turn (it was six).

- **Relics: the old outlaws for sale (1907)** (`[World] Relics`, on; settings version 315; FIVEDAY reserve R26). In John's 1907, the photographers of Saint Denis and Blackwater think of the trade in the old outlaws: cabinet cards of dead bandits laid out on a door, an old man paid a dollar to pose with a Winchester, the hanging postcards that sell best. Near the studio, once a town a day, the box shows the window, where one face is one you'd know.
  - Why it reads true: the West was sold as nostalgia while it was still dying. For a man who outlived his time, finding his past at ten cents a card is grief for a world rather than a person.

- **The boy who remembers (1907)** (`[World] BoyRemembers`, on, with EpilogueCarries; settings version 314; FIVEDAY reserve R25). In John's 1907, a carried-over man who was a boy of eight to fifteen in '99 and met Arthur remembers him as a boy remembers a man: the dollar outside the store, the reins of the bay horse, the way he stood with his thumbs in his belt. If John comes within six metres, the young man asks him aloud, once, whether he knew a man called Morgan.
  - Why it reads true: an admired grown-up becomes the template a boy measures himself against for years, and the memory keeps the man's best and loses the rest.

- **The claim agent and the widow** (`[World] ClaimAgents`, on; settings version 313; FIVEDAY reserve R24). Three days after a man dies at the Annesburg mine, the company's claim agent calls on his widow with fifty dollars and a paper, "in full settlement". After a killing in Saint Denis, the insurance man calls instead, to ask about the policy rather than about her. The box tells it, and the next day what she did: signed (four in ten), sent him away and wrote to a lawyer (three in ten), or the town took up a collection so she needn't sign (three in ten).
  - Why it reads true: the companies settled for what a grieving family would take, timing the visit for when grief was rawest. The collection is the town closing ranks.

- **Grinding season in the cane** (`[World] GrindingSeason`, on; settings version 312; FIVEDAY reserve R23). From 15 October to 20 December the sugar mills of Lemoyne run day and night. The working men of Rhodes, Saint Denis and Lagras work into the night, with less saloon and less home, and think of it: the knives, the rollers that took a man's arm, the molasses smoke, the sleep they aren't getting. With TownEvents, it ends with the grinding ball at Rhodes on the twentieth, where a couple dance every reel and come away courting.
  - Why it reads true: the grinding was the hardest and most dangerous labour of the Southern year, and exhaustion narrows a mind to the next shift. The ball at the end is the release that made it bearable.
  - Offline, a working man at 9 pm in a cane town is at work 95% of the time in the grinding (0% before). A Testing row stages the ball.

- **A fais do-do in Lagras** (`[World] EventFaisDoDo`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 311; FIVEDAY reserve R22). Every Saturday night in Lagras, from eight till half past eleven, the bayou's dance: a fiddle and an accordion, waltzes and two-steps on the boards, the gumbo pot passed round, the babies carried to the back room and sung to sleep ("fais do-do"). A young couple of the village dance every dance together, and come away courting; the aunts are talking by morning.
  - Why it reads true: the weekly dance was the bayou's whole social world, where courtship was done in public, under every aunt's eye, and so safely.
  - A Testing row, "A fais do-do", stages one.

- **A brass band funeral in Saint Denis** (`[World] EventBrassFuneral`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 310; FIVEDAY reserve R21). A day or two after a named death in Saint Denis, from three till five, posted the day before, from the cemetery spot (else near you):
  - the society's brass band leading the dirge out, its men in black sashes behind the dead;
  - the hymn at the graveside, with the trombone carrying the tune;
  - "Go on home now", and then the snare cracks and the second line dances back through the street.
  - Being there warms the regulars to you, and the morning has the word.
  - Why it reads true: the city's benevolent societies buried their members with a band, mourning on the way out and rejoicing on the way back: grief turned into a whole street's celebration of a life.
  - A Testing row, "A brass band funeral", stages one.

- **Murder ballads at a fire** (`[Scenes] MurderBallads`, on; settings version 309; FIVEDAY reserve R20). Sit by a campfire out of the towns at night, and once a night a voice from the next fire sings a murder ballad on the ticker, an opening and four verses. It's your own most-told killing if one has been told of ("A stranger rode in on a cold grey morn..."), else a death RealNPCs knows, with its name, its place and its hand. Nothing is spawned: it's heard from the dark.
  - Why it reads true: the murder ballad was how the back country kept its news and its morals, a real killing sung until the facts wore off and the warning stayed. Hearing your own deed sung by someone who doesn't know you're listening is a reputation's strangest mirror.
  - A Testing row, "A murder ballad from the next fire", sings one.

- **Mardi Gras night in Saint Denis** (`[World] EventMardiGras`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 308; FIVEDAY reserve R19). On Fat Tuesday (14 February in 1899), from seven till eleven on the Saint Denis main street spot (else near you), posted the day before.
  - Masks and torches and a cornet band, and the krewe's float with paper dragons and a Devil throwing beads.
  - A townsman crowned King of the Carnival, and the king cake cut, with a townswoman finding the baby (next year's queen). Then dancing till the bells.
  - The morning is Ash Wednesday, and very quiet.
  - Why it reads true: the carnival is licensed disorder, one night when rank and rule are turned over by permission. That's why a strict town needs it, and why the morning after is so quiet.
  - A Testing row, "Mardi Gras", stages it.

- **The Fourth of July as an event** (`[World] EventFourth`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 307; FIVEDAY reserve R18). On the Fourth, from six till half past nine, at the bandstand spot (else near you) in Valentine, Saint Denis, Rhodes, Blackwater, Strawberry and Armadillo, posted the day before: the band and the bunting, the Declaration read out, a speech that goes on, firecrackers under the horses, the shooting match (a townsman's win), and the fireworks at dark. Being there warms the regulars to you, and the morning has the word, and which roof caught fire.
  - Why it reads true: thirty-four years after the war, the Fourth was the one day the whole country agreed to be one town, a ritual of belonging, and of gunpowder.
  - A Testing row, "The Fourth of July", stages it.

- **The county fair** (`[World] EventFair`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 305; FIVEDAY reserve R17). At Valentine on the third Saturday of September, from eleven till two, at the stockyards spot (else near you), posted the day before. The bunting and the cornet; the livestock judged, and a townsman's prize beast named; the blue ribbon for pies to a townswoman, and a rival's sour face; the greasy pig.
  - A neighbour is warmer to the prize man, a rival woman envies the ribbon, and being there warms the regulars to you. The morning has who won, and whose cousins the judges were.
  - Why it reads true: the fair was the county's yearly show of itself, and a ribbon was status the whole county saw.
  - The festivals (the fair here; the Fourth and Mardi Gras next) share one module: booked from the calendar, staged with a crowd out of view, settled with their winners. A Testing row, "The county fair", stages it.

- **Town base ball: Valentine against Strawberry** (`[World] EventBaseBall`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 304; FIVEDAY reserve R16). Every other Saturday from April to September, at two, the two towns' nines play, at home by turns, posted on the Thursday. It plays at the home town's showground spot, else near you: the pitches, the hits and the strikes, the crowd's shouts, the score after the third, sixth and ninth, the result.
  - The losers' men are sore at the winners', and a game there warms the home regulars to you a little. The season's tally is kept, and the morning has the result.
  - Why it reads true: a neighbouring town is the best rival there is. In-group pride and out-group rivalry cost nothing to join, and they bind a town together.
  - Offline: twelve games over half a year (before: none). A Testing row, "Base ball", stages one.

- **The telegraph clerk knows first** (`[Mind] TelegraphKnows`, on; settings version 303; FIVEDAY reserve R15). The station agent at his counter thinks of what came over the wire these two days: deaths in other towns, by name ("Nobody here knows yet. I'm not paid to tell them."). With you at his window, he thinks of your deed in another town before the word has reached his ("Wire from Valentine about a stranger: SHOT DEAD STOP. And here he stands at my window.").
  - Why it reads true: the clerk of 1899 held the town's news an hour before the town did. His trade was discretion and his temptation was to break it.

- **The editor takes a side** (`[World] EditorSides`, on; settings version 302; FIVEDAY reserve R14). Every town's paper has its editor and his line:
  - law and order (Saint Denis, Rhodes): hang the man, the sheriff must act;
  - the people's (Valentine, Strawberry, Annesburg): ask who the real thieves are;
  - business (Blackwater, Armadillo): capital won't settle where lead flies.
  - When one of your deeds is printed, the editor adds his word in that slant. For two days, someone near you in that town who read it agrees, or argues with the paper (the bold and the cold do).
  - Why it reads true: 1899's town papers were partisan by trade. The framing decided whether a killing was a crime or rough justice, and readers took it as fact or as "the editor" by their own lean.

- **Posters that drift from the rumours** (`[World] DriftingPosters`, on; settings version 301; FIVEDAY reserve R13). In a town your deed's rumour has reached, the first time each day, the box has the bill outside the sheriff's as it reads there.
  - It's true where the telling is still faithful: your name, a big man, a dark coat.
  - One thing is wrong further out: a gray coat, a white horse, one more dead.
  - It's wild once the story has been passed along enough: seven foot, a scar, twice the dead, a name that isn't yours.
  - Now and then someone near holds you up against it ("Poster says seven foot. He's not seven foot.").
  - Why it reads true: each retelling levels the detail and sharpens the drama (serial reproduction), so a town's picture of a man is the story it has heard, not the man.
  - Offline: across tellings from 0 to 100 fidelity, 21% of bills name you rightly (before: no bill of ours).

- **Dime novels about you** (`[World] DimeNovels`, on; settings version 300; FIVEDAY reserve R12). When your most-told deed has been talked of in two towns or more, some New York hack writes it up. A dime novel turns up in the store windows on a Monday, and the box has its lurid title ("ARTHUR MORGAN, THE SCOURGE OF VALENTINE; or, Forty Men in the Snow"). Once a deed, sixty days apart.
  - For thirty days, people near you in town think of having read it. The bold admire it, the fearful believe every word, the cold scoff at it, the rest are tickled, and a few think the man himself looks smaller than the woodcut.
  - Why it reads true: real outlaws read about themselves in the dime novels, and a legend outruns its man. The town reacts to the story, not the person, and the original is always a let-down.
  - A Testing row, "Publish a dime novel of your deeds", publishes one (a test deed if none has travelled, never added to your deeds).

- **Memorial photographs** (`[Mind] MemorialPhotos`, on; settings version 299; FIVEDAY reserve R11). In Saint Denis and Blackwater, the photographer at his counter thinks of the memorial portraits he made of the town's dead these ten days, by name: posed among the family on a brace, eyes painted on the plate, a hand on the Bible. A busy spell gets a thought of its own. A mourner there thinks of the photograph that came about a time in three (in place of a mourning thought).
  - Why it reads true: the memorial photograph was often the only likeness a poor family ever had of its dead, and keeping them present is how grief settles rather than ends.
  - Offline: a third of mourners' thoughts there are of the photograph, and the photographer's counter thoughts name the dead (before: neither).

- **The undertaker thinks of the week's dead** (`[Mind] UndertakerKnows`, on; settings version 298; FIVEDAY reserve R10). The undertaker at his counter (Rhodes, Armadillo) thinks of his town's dead of the last seven days, by name and by how they died: the fever's closed box, what the mine sent up, the rope's mark, the lead dug out for the sheriff, the one who did it himself, or the one you sent him. A busy week is a thought of its own, and when you've killed twice or more, he thinks of you as trade. In a quiet week, his trade's thoughts as before.
  - Why it reads true: he handles every death, so he keeps the town's real count, and his gallows humour is how a man stands measuring his neighbours.
  - Offline: in a week with deaths in his town, every one of his counter thoughts names the dead, the week or you (before: his trade's thoughts only).

- **Fever in town** (`[World] Fever` and `FeverDeaths`, both on; settings version 297; FIVEDAY reserve R9). Rarely (a town in about a hundred and fifty a day, ninety days apart), diphtheria, typhoid or scarlet fever breaks out in a town. Each day it passes along the warm ties of its residents (friends, sweethearts, the neighbours who visit), one tie in twenty-five a day.
  - The sick keep to home for about a week, at any hour, and think of it. People near you in that town think of the fear.
  - At three sick, the board of health posts a quarantine (the box). When nobody's sick it's over, and the box says what it cost.
  - With FeverDeaths, one in twelve of the sick dies of it, named and mourned like anyone (the wake, the grave).
  - Why it reads true: a disease that spreads by kindness, along the visits and the shared pew, turns every friend's call into a gamble.
  - Offline, an outbreak through a town of sixty takes about twelve sick and one or two dead and lasts up to six weeks, with the quarantine posted in three outbreaks in five. A sick townsman at noon goes home 96% of the time (before: no fever). A Testing row, "A fever starts in this town", starts one.

- **The quilting bee, overheard** (`[Scenes] QuiltingBee`, on; settings version 296; FIVEDAY reserve R8). On Tuesday and Thursday afternoons, stand still on foot by a town's church steps (the church hall's window is open) and for half a minute the ticker carries "Through the window": the needles and the kettle, then the town's goings-on of the last three days by name, as the women tell them (who quarrelled, who's courting, who came to blows). Their own talk runs between, and a word about you by what the town knows of you: feared, warm, or a stranger to them. Once a town a day.
  - Why it reads true: the quilting bee held a small town's social knowledge, and gossip is how a town keeps its norms. They're candid because they don't know you can hear.
  - Offline: half a minute tells you two of the town's goings-on by name and what it thinks of you. A Testing row, "Overhear the quilting bee", plays it.

- **A barn raising** (`[World] EventBarnRaisings`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 295; FIVEDAY reserve R7). From March to October, now and then a farm family in a country town has its barn raised by the neighbours on a Saturday from nine, posted ahead. It plays at the town's corral spot, else near you: the call, the bents going up on the pike poles, the dinner on trestles, the family's thanks.
  - If two townsmen are at odds, the foreman puts them on the same beam. They bicker, and then the beam has to go up: six times in ten it mends the feud (a makeup), and the rest they fall out again.
  - The neighbours who came are warmer to the family, and the regulars to you if you were there.
  - Why it reads true: a barn was more than one family could raise, so the district did it. And a goal neither side can reach alone is the oldest cure for a feud there is (Sherif's superordinate goals): not certain, but better odds than talking.
  - Offline, 58% of feuding pairs put on one beam come away mended (before: no barn raisings). A Testing row, "A barn raising", stages one.

- **A box social** (`[World] EventBoxSocials`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 294; FIVEDAY reserve R6). On the first Saturday of the month, posted the Thursday before, the church in each settled town holds a box social from three. It plays at the church steps spot, else near you.
  - A young woman's supper box is auctioned unopened, and everyone knows whose it is. Two young men of the town bid each other up for it, the one she's already warm to among them.
  - The winner and she come away courting, and the loser envies him.
  - While they bid, J lets you go fifty cents over. Buy it and she eats with you: she thinks the more of you, and the suitor you outbid is sore at you.
  - Why it reads true: courtship in public, with money as the signal and the whole town watching. Costly signalling and rivalry at their plainest.
  - Offline, once a month in each of the six towns with the cast, each a new courting tie (before: none). A Testing row, "A box social", stages one.

- **A river baptism** (`[World] EventBaptisms`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 293; FIVEDAY reserve R5). The Sunday after a camp meeting saves a man, he's baptised in the river from two in the afternoon, at the town's new "riverbank" spot (the spots tool has the kind now). The preacher and the faithful stand on the bank: the hymn, the preacher's words, under and up, the amens, his words and his thought.
  - Baptised, he keeps from the saloon three weeks and doesn't backslide. Offline, of men saved at a camp meeting, 67% are still saved on day ten without it and 100% with it.
  - Without a riverbank spot, or with you away, it happens all the same, and the morning has the word. A while there warms the regulars to you.
  - Why it reads true: a commitment made twice, and in public, is far harder to walk back. That's why the churches insisted on the river.
  - A Testing row, "A baptism at the river", stages one.

- **Camp meetings: three nights of revival** (`[World] EventRevivals`, on, with TownEvents; settings version 292; FIVEDAY reserve R4). Now and then from June to August, a travelling preacher holds a camp meeting outside one of the country towns (Valentine, Strawberry, Rhodes, Blackwater, Annesburg, Armadillo, Emerald Ranch). It runs three nights, half past seven till ten, posted ahead.
  - It plays at the town's showground or church steps spot (else a spot near you): the preacher in a ring of the faithful, the hymns, the amens. The first night is the call to repent, the second hellfire, the third the altar call.
  - On the third night, a man of the town whose worry is the drink or the cards goes up to the mourners' bench. For two weeks he keeps from the saloon and the card table and leans to church, though a third of them backslide at the week. Offline, a saved drinker's 7 pm saloon goes from 18% to 0%.
  - A night there warms the regulars to you a little, and the morning has the word.
  - Why it reads true: a public vow under a crowd's eye is one of the strongest pulls on behaviour there is, and it fades when the crowd goes home. That's why the backsliding was as famous as the saving.
  - Offline, about twelve camp meetings a summer across the seven towns (before: none). Two Testing rows stage night one and the altar call.

- **A sheriff's sale in the street** (`[World] Foreclosures`, on; settings version 291; FIVEDAY reserve R3). On the first day a resident's worry over the bank ends badly, one time in four, the bank sells them up in public. From ten till two, with the town's bankdoor (or mainstreet) spot recorded and you near, the sheriff's man calls the street together and sells three of their things: the plough, the milch cow, the parlour organ.
  - The neighbours bid low. Two sales in five, not one of them will bid against the family, and the bank's man takes each lot.
  - The family watches if they're near, and thinks of it.
  - While a lot is called, J buys it back for them, once a sale: the family's thanks and a kindness the whole street sees. Half of these families still leave on the train after, as before.
  - Why it reads true: the 1890s were the farm-mortgage decade the Farmers' Alliance came out of. A public sale was shame for the family and fear for every neighbour holding a note at the same bank, and a refusal to bid was a protest a whole town could make without a word.
  - Offline, a town of sixty residents has about twelve a game year (before: none). A Testing row, "A sheriff's sale in the street", stages one.

- **The slate at the store** (`[Scenes] StoreSlate`, on; settings version 290; FIVEDAY reserve R2). When you're at the store, now and then one of the town's people goes up to the counter and asks to put it on the slate. What they owe is their own this week, more with debt, the cards, the bank or the drink on their mind, and nothing for the well-off.
  - The keeper chalks it up, asks for something on account first, or says the slate's full and it's cash from now on. That's by what's owed and his own manner.
  - Refused, they think of the shame in front of the store. For ten seconds, J (the event key) lets you pay what they owe from your own pocket: their thanks, the keeper's, and a kindness seen by everyone near.
  - On Saturday afternoon, payday, they come to pay it down instead.
  - Why it reads true: credit is a relationship before it's money. The storekeeper's book is where a small town keeps score of who is trusted, and a refusal at the counter is a public shame.
  - Offline, of 3,000 asking: 53% chalked, 14% asked for something first, 32% told it's full. So J is offered 46% of the time (before: no slate). A Testing row, "Someone asks for credit at the store", shows a refusal.

- **Drummers on the train** (`[World] Drummers`, on, with TrainTime; settings version 289; FIVEDAY reserve R1). One man in three who steps down off a train is now a drummer: a travelling salesman for a far city's firm, with a line of goods (hardware, dry goods, cigars, notions, stoves, barbed wire, sewing machines, canned goods). The box says he's at the hotel.
  - When you're near the store with him in town, he walks over to its keeper with his pitch. The keeper orders, haggles or sends him off by his own manner, and the drummer thinks of it after. An order makes the Around town box.
  - He thinks a drummer's thoughts (the hotel beds, the sample case, the next town), is never one of the regulars, and is gone on the train in two days.
  - Why it reads true: the drummer was a small town's news from outside, and its storekeeper's order from him decided what the town could buy for a season. He lived on being told no, and it shows.
  - Offline: of 1,000 men off a train, 35% are drummers (before: none). A Testing row, "A drummer calls at the store", plays the call.

- **More of the moment in what people think** (`[Mind] MoreMoments`, on; settings version 288; FIVEDAY item 32e; roadmap's moments still to do). New moments:
  - a horse bolting under its rider (loud: thought before anything else);
  - a purse lifted, picked or taken in the last hour and a half (they know it's gone even when they never saw who);
  - standing in a fistfight's ring;
  - being near an execution by the road;
  - rain on them in the saddle;
  - a thunderstorm out of doors.
  - Women in a fistfight think their own lines where the fist pools were a man's. How you look beside them has pools of its own, half the time.
  - Not built: the hat lost. No read of a hat is proven, and the clothes reads are held back.
  - Offline, of people read riding or walking with a fifth in rain, 14% now think of one of the new moments (before, none).

- **Someone else pulls up at the broken wagon** (`[Encounters] WagonOthersStop`, on, with WagonWheel; settings version 287; FIVEDAY item 32d; README 32). Twenty-five seconds after the driver is down beside his wagon and you haven't put your shoulder to it, or once you've ridden on, six wagons in ten get a passer-by: the nearest free man on the road, on foot or on horseback, turns aside to it. He offers a hand (he says so if he saw you ride past) and sets the wheel with the driver. Then the driver thanks him and drives on, and the helper thinks of it. If you get there first, he goes on his way.
  - Why it reads true: on an empty road the bystander effect runs backwards. With nobody else to leave it to, the one passing feels the whole duty. And a norm broken in plain sight (your riding past) makes the next man keener to keep it.
  - Offline: of 1,000 wagons left standing, 58% now get someone else (before: none).

- **A stalker on horseback who rides** (`[Stalkers] StalkerRides`, on; settings version 286; FIVEDAY item 32c; README 13). A mounted stalker used to sit his horse watching and get down to do anything else. Now he stays in the saddle to follow you at a distance and to ride in close and back out round you when you stand still. He pulls up a horse's length short when he rushes you, falls back to sixty metres when you move, and rides off when he flees.
  - The follow, circle and rush use the go-to-entity the allies already ride with, which is proven to drive the horse. The flee and the fall-back give the game's smart flee from the saddle, which isn't proven. So every ride is checked: if the horse hasn't moved three metres in five seconds, he gets down and does it on foot as before.
  - Offline: of the stalker's eleven modes, a mounted one now gets down for 3, where before it was 8.
  - A Testing row, "A stalker on horseback now", sends one.

- **Kin take up a wrong one townsman did another** (`[Scenes] KinTownWrongs`, on, with KinGrudges; settings version 285; FIVEDAY item 32b; README 20). When one townsman beats or robs another, two times in five a man of the victim's family (a brother, father, son, cousin or uncle) comes to town five to nine days later. If the one who did it is near you, the kin walks up to him, says who he is and where he rode in from, and has it out. The man answers by his nature: hard words, a walk-off, an apology, or blows between two hot-tempered men. A notice names them.
  - Before, only your own wrongs brought the kin, and a townsman's wrongs were taken up only by friends who happened to be near. Offline, of 200 beatings and thefts on people with a brother, 70 now bring him to town (before: none).
  - A Testing row, "Kin come for a townsman who beat theirs", books one due now between two people near you.

- **A gambler borrows from a man of his town** (`[Mind] GamblersBorrow`, on; settings version 284; FIVEDAY item 32a; README 19). The cards worry now has a real lender, like a debt has its creditor: a man of the gambler's town, a friend first. The two meet in the street over it as it goes on.
  - He asks for ten dollars, then five to win it back, then begs. A kind man lends, a cold one won't, and a hothead snaps at him (offline, across the seven tempers: 28% lend, 42% refuse, 28% snap).
  - At the end he pays it back where the street can see, or it's called in and never paid. The lender thinks of it too: his own lines, where before an unknown link kind fell to the courting ones.
  - Offline, 3% of a town's men carry the cards worry on a given day, and each now has a lender to meet (before, none did).
  - A Testing row, "A gambler asks a man for a loan", plays it.

- **Fence cutters** (`[World] FenceCutters`, on; settings version 283; FIVEDAY item 31). About once a fortnight, a homesteader in an open-range town (Valentine, Strawberry, Rhodes, Emerald Ranch, Blackwater, Armadillo, Tumbleweed) finds his wire cut at first light. He blames the cattleman: his feud neighbour if he has one, else a ranch man of the town.
  - Their tie goes two steps colder. With MindPlans he means to get even, so the feud climbs into the quarrels and fights the ties already drive. Both men think of it, and the town talks of it. Offline, a pair one quarrel apart goes from -1 to -3.
  - He's right two times in three. The third time it was drifters cutting through for water. If you were near the town that night, the Around town box says what you saw, so you know what he doesn't. If you're in the town that morning, the box has his word.
  - Why it reads true: wire was cut at night by men nobody saw, so a wrong with no face takes the face of an old grudge (the fundamental attribution error, with confirmation bias on top).
  - A Testing row cuts one in the town you're in now.

- **Sale day at the Valentine stockyards** (`[World] SaleDay`, on; settings version 282; FIVEDAY item 30). On Tuesdays from ten to half past one, with the stockyards spot recorded and you near it, the named men about the pens gather to the auctioneer. He calls three lots (four in the autumn sale, September to November), two buyers bid on each, and the boldest gets it.
  - One sale in five (two in five in the autumn glut) ends with a lot nobody wants. It goes for half its worth, and its rancher is ruined: his thought, a neighbour's, the price in the Around town box, and, with WorriesShown and no worry of his own, the debt worry bending his days for a week. That worry is kept for the session only, so it's gone when you reload.
  - Everyone who was there carries the day into the afternoon. From one o'clock the drovers drink their pay (offline, 37% of working men by a saloon at three go in, against none before), and the ruined man drinks too (23%).
  - Why it reads true: a year's work sold in a minute, in public. A loss weighs more than the same gain, so one bad lot outweighs the good ones. Money that comes all at once goes all at once.
  - A Testing row starts one now.

- **The shape-up at the Saint Denis wharf** (`[World] ShapeUp`, on; settings version 281; FIVEDAY item 29). At dawn in Saint Denis, with the wharf spot recorded and you near it, the named men about the wharf now crowd round the foreman and he picks his gang: about half, the steadier first.
  - The picked think of the wage and go to work.
  - The passed-over think of the shame and drift to the saloon from seven in the morning. Offline, 39% of them went in by ten, where none did before.
  - The angriest of the passed-over may have it out with the last man picked: hard words at once, and a rematch plan between them (with MindPlans). Offline, this happens on 42% of mornings.
  - A Testing row starts one now.

- **Three whistles** (`[World] MineWhistle`, on; settings version 280; FIVEDAY item 28). Rarely, the mine whistle at Annesburg blows three times: about eight times in a game year, never within three weeks of the last. One or two of the town's men are killed below, a miner first. They're named, never shown, and mourned like anyone (their friends hear of it, and the wake is kept).
  - If you're in Annesburg, you hear it. People near you run for the pithead (when its spot is recorded), a wife waits on the names, and the names come up from the shaft. Then comes anger at the company, and the hat goes round for the widows.
  - Away, the word reaches you.
  - A Testing row sets one off now.

- **The wake** (`[World] Wakes`, on; settings version 279; FIVEDAY item 27). The night after a death in a town, or the night before one of enRAGE's funerals, the neighbours now sit up with the dead from eight to two. The Around town box tells you once a night (lamps in every window, neighbours at the door with covered dishes), and half the street's quiet talks that night are of the dead. In West World, one of the waking near you swears the dead one moved. It plays outside and in talk, since interiors are out of reach.

- **Mourning and graves visited** (`[World] Mourning`, on; settings version 278; FIVEDAY item 26). For three weeks after a friend's death, people mourn:
  - Their days go quieter: home, prayer and the church, not the cards or the saloon. Offline at 7 pm, 11% chose home, prayer or church before and 38% now; the cards, the saloon or showing off went from 22% to 4%.
  - They have their own thoughts of the dead, and neighbours near them hush for it.
  - On a Sunday afternoon one walks to the grave and kneels, using the game's own graveside kneel. The grave is the town's recorded cemetery spot, or the churchyard where none is recorded.
  - At the grave of someone you killed, if you come near, they know you.

- **Sunday, properly** (`[World] SundayMorning`, on; settings version 277; FIVEDAY item 25). The whole Sunday morning now plays out:
  - From eight the town's believers make for the church, the devout most. Offline, 42% choose the nine o'clock service, where 19% did before.
  - From half past nine, near the church, you hear the hymn from the street; the hymns are old, and only their names are used.
  - The sermon is on the town's week: a killing known there, a theft, a death among them, or someone gone to drink. The churchgoers think it over afterwards ("The reverend preached on the Sixth Commandment. Everybody knew who he meant.").
  - From noon, dinner on the grounds is in the Around town box, and neighbours linger to eat and talk.

- **Lasting marks** (`[Mind] LastingMarks`, on; `LastingMarksLook`, off; settings version 276; FIVEDAY item 24). Someone you beat now carries it for days: four after a beating, one after a knocking down. When you're about, they think of it ("Every step, I feel where he put his boot."), and a friend of theirs near you names you for it ("That's the one did Hank's face. Look at him.").
  - Showing it on the body, as an injured gait and bruising, sits behind `LastingMarksLook`, off until you've tried the probe. The game's names for these aren't proven, so the Testing row "A beating's marks" tries the next gait and damage pack on the nearest named person each press and says which.
  - Set the ones that read in `LastingMarksGait` and `LastingMarksPack`.

- **Gossip that travels along ties** (`[Mind] TieGossip`, on; settings version 275; FIVEDAY item 23). Inside a town, word of your violence and your thieving now moves person to person along the ties the street made. The friends of someone who knows hear it the next day, and anyone else they're tied to the day after, a little more bent at each telling. Whoever hears it thinks of who told them and what they got ("Abner swears a gunman nobody knew beat Eli Pike half to death..."). Offline, in a town of 30: 10 know by the first day, 19 by the second, 26 by the third and 29 by the fourth; before, it stayed at the 6 who saw it or were told that day. A week on, the talk is done.

- **Shared fright** (`[Mind] SharedFright`, on; settings version 274; FIVEDAY item 22). Two people who both saw you kill are now tied by it. The tie between them warms a step, and for two weeks they keep clear of you together when both are about. When the street's quiet talk falls to the two of them, it's about that three times in four, low-voiced: "You were there. You saw it." "Don't. Not here."

- **Survivors come back changed** (`[Mind] SurvivorsChanged`, on; settings version 273; FIVEDAY item 21). Someone you beat or robbed and left alive now comes back changed, a day on, by who they are:
  - The angry with friends bring up to two of them when they call you out. The friends back them up in words and fight with them.
  - The bold have more standing for having faced you.
  - The timid and the cowards are broken by it. They never come at you, and they keep clear of you and off the street.
  - The rest let it lie.

  Their thoughts say which, and so do a friend's near them. Offline: 27% broken, 21% get even, 20% stand taller, 31% let it lie.

- **Trust lost fast, won slow** (`[Mind] TrustSlow`, on; settings version 272; FIVEDAY item 20). A shove, a beating, a robbery or a theft of yours now takes three greetings' worth off that person's liking at once. For 60 game days after, your greetings count for less with them: a fifth the day after, with the rest coming back over three weeks. The forgiving come round sooner and the unforgiving later, and each kindness since helps. Offline, a friend at a full bar whom you then knock down takes seven daily hellos to come back (five for the forgiving, eight for the unforgiving). Anyone you never wronged still fills in three hellos.

- **The town moves on while you're away** (`[World] WhileAway`, on; settings version 271; FIVEDAY item 19). Ride back into a town after two game days or more and you hear what you missed. The Around town box gets a "What you missed" item, and someone near who knows you thinks about telling you. It covers up to five things, the weightiest first: deaths, weddings, a hanging, the election's winner, the trial's verdict, who left on the train, a new face at a counter, the show, the dance, the prize fight's winner, and someone taken to drink or always at the church. It starts counting from your first visit to each town after updating.

- **Peak and end** (`[Mind] PeakEnd`, on; settings version 270; FIVEDAY item 18). A meeting with you is now remembered by its strongest moment and how it ended, not by how long it went on. A meeting means what someone feels about you, with no more than a quarter hour between. A long friendly talk ended by a shove is remembered as the shove, and a bad meeting put right at the end is remembered for both.

- **Stress that builds, to a breaking point** (`[Mind] StressBuilds`, on; settings version 269; FIVEDAY item 17). Until now strain lasted a day: someone snapped only when two troubles landed at once. Now a week's troubles add up, person by person: a friend's death, a death seen, a beating, a theft, quarrels, and a worry that's deepening or at its worst. Past their breaking point, which comes lower for the anxious and higher for the steady, a person breaks in a way that fits them, and the habit stays a week:
  - drinks the nights away;
  - prays at the church;
  - keeps going back to the grave;
  - takes it out on those at home (a married man with a temper; the domestic dispute picks him first, and he snaps at whoever's near);
  - takes the train the next day.

  Each way has its own thoughts. The same person breaks at most once in three weeks.

- **The notice board** (`[World] NoticeBoard`, on; settings version 268; FIVEDAY item 16). Until now each of a town's days was posted on its own as it came: a toast for a dance, a clipping for an election or the show's bill. Now they're gathered on one board. The reader has a new page, "Notice board", listing what's coming in the next seven days, the town you're in first and then each town by day: dances, elections, the circuit judge and hangings, the Wild West show, prize fights, announced weddings, enRAGE's funerals, and the town's Saturday wages and Sunday service. And when you come into a town that has something posted, the Around town box says so, once a town a day. Shivarees are left off, since they come as a surprise.

- **Pinkertons asking** (`[Encounters] Pinkertons`, on; settings version 267; FIVEDAY item 15, from roadmap.md's idea bank: "a man in a bowler asks regulars about the player"). By day in a town where a violent deed of yours is known from the past week, now and then (a town's day in four, at most once a town in five days) a man in a city suit walks in from out of view and asks two or three of the town's people near you after "a man of your description". Your friends lie for you, those afraid of you tell him, the law-minded tell him too, the rest shrug. He thanks the last and walks off. Afterwards a friend who comes over warns you that he was asking, and names whoever talked. A Testing row sends one now.

- **Witnesses who choose** (`[World] WitnessesChoose`, on; settings version 266; FIVEDAY item 14, from roadmap.md's idea bank: "after a crime, threaten or pay a named witness. The frightened tell a different story; the paid say nothing"). Until now everyone who saw you do violence told it an hour on, if they lived. Now each witness RealNPCs knows chooses: tell it, or keep quiet, out of fear of you or loyalty to you. The law-abiding, the honest and a friend of the one you hurt tell. If nobody who saw it will tell, the town never learns it was you, as if they'd all died. And within the hour, a gun on a witness leans on them: the frightened swear they saw nothing ("My eyes are bad. Everybody knows it."), the proud look you in the eye and tell it all the sooner. Why it reads true: a witness to a killing in a small town weighs the cost of speaking; fear and loyalty silence, a tie to the victim speaks up, and threats work on the anxious and backfire on the proud. Offline, of a crowd that saw it, one in ten keep quiet with nothing between them and you, one in three if they're afraid of you. Not yet seen in game.

- **A shivaree the night after a wedding** (`[World] EventShivarees`, on, with `[World] TownEvents`; settings version 265; FIVEDAY item 13, from roadmap.md's idea bank: "the night after a wedding, friends bang pots and pans under the couple's window until the groom stands drinks"). The night after a wedding in a town, from nine, the couple's friends gather with pans, a cowbell and a tin horn and shout for the groom ("No sleep for you two till the drinks are stood!") until he comes out, groans, and stands them all a round. Be there a minute and the town's regulars think a little better of you for it; away or there, the couple's friends come out of it warmer to them, and the town has the story next morning. Why it reads true: the charivari came west from French and English villages, and on the frontier it was the town's claim on a new couple: noise as a welcome and a test of good humour, paid off in drink. Testing row "A shivaree". Not yet seen in game.

- **The temperance ladies** (`[Scenes] Temperance`, on; settings version 264; FIVEDAY item 12, from roadmap.md's idea bank: "women pray and sing outside the saloon. Drinkers jeer or slink off; one husband is marched home"). By day in the street, a woman of the temperance cause stops a man bound for the saloon and puts the pledge to him ("Every glass in there is bought with bread money."). He jeers and goes in anyway, slinks off ashamed, swears off for the day, or is taken home on her arm. The pious start it oftener, and a man whose worry is the drink is the one she picks. Whoever saw it thinks of it after. Why it reads true: the Woman's Christian Temperance Union was the largest women's organisation in the country by 1899, and its members met drinkers at the saloon door with prayer and pledge cards; spite and shame were what they mostly got, and now and then a man who signed. Not yet seen in game.

- **Payday Saturday** (`[Scenes] Payday`, on; settings version 263; FIVEDAY item 11, from roadmap.md's idea bank: "Saturday afternoon pay fills the saloon ... Debts are called in, and wives wait at the saloon door"; the debts already come due between the real creditor and debtor whenever they meet, the street's strongest reason, so payday adds nothing to them). From noon till eight on a Saturday in town, the wages are out and it shows: the working people head for the saloon, the card table, the store or a drink instead of the work that stopped at noon; and a woman may stop a man on his way to the saloon for the week's money ("The pay, before it's gone through that door."), and get all of it, half, a promise, or his back. Why it reads true: a weekly pay packet's first hours are its loosest, as every saloon keeper, card sharp and creditor of 1899 knew. Offline, working people choosing to spend at 2 pm: Friday none, Saturday about a third. Not yet seen in game.

- **A prize fight out back of the saloon** (`[World] EventPrizeFights`, on, with `[World] TownEvents`; settings version 262; FIVEDAY item 10, from roadmap.md's idea bank: "an illegal bare-knuckle fight in a barn, bets, a ring of regulars. Bet, fight, or tip off the sheriff"). Now and then, at most once in six days a town, two of a town's men of a fighting temper agree to settle it with their fists for a purse after dark. It's never in the paper: from three that afternoon the word goes round ("A $7 purse. Keep it from the marshal."). Be there after nine and it's out back of the saloon you last came out of: a ring of men, a lookout watching the street, the rules, then the bout, the ring shouting, until one man is down to half his strength. Press J: put a dollar on either man (two back if he wins), slip away and tip off the sheriff (the lookout cries the law a little later and they scatter, nobody knowing who told), or just watch. Away, it's settled by the two men's natures, and the town has the result next morning. The two remember it, and a fair fight leaves respect between them, not a grudge. Why it reads true: bare-knuckle fighting was against the law in most states by 1899 and drew crowds all the more for it; in a small town it was two men's reputations, a hat passed round and a boy watching for the marshal. Testing row "A prize fight out back". Not yet seen in game.

- **About a thousand more written lines where a long ride repeats most** (FIVEDAY item 9; Lex, 2026-11-04: "thousands of thoughts ... already proven"). Measured first: over three hours of riding between towns, the thought heard most often again was what people think of you as you pass (how you sit your horse, your hat, your gun), then the hour of the day, then seeing you again, the secret, the toothache and the drink as worries, what they just saw and the weather. Those pools are now about three times deeper: what townspeople notice about you on foot, mounted or with a gun out (55, 55 and 40 lines became 170, 170 and 125), seeing you again (55 to 170), dawn, day, evening and night, each meal and the small hours, each day of the week (Monday's wash, Saturday's pay, Sunday's rest), every stage of the secret, the bad tooth and the drink, every kind of thing seen in the street and every weather. For players without the model above all. After: in the third hour of a ride, a thought already seen went from 22% to 11%. Lines only.

- **The encounters speak in their own voices** (`[Mind] VoicedEncounters`, on, with `[Mind] VoicedDialogue` and the voice server running; settings version 261; FIVEDAY item 8, from the plan Lex asked for on 2026-11-05: "more of what's said near you in cloned voices"). The street's scenes and the shouts of a brawl were already said in the speaker's own cloned voice; the encounters and walk-ups weren't: a friend's visit, a warning, a traveller asking the way, the men at a campfire, a quarrel at a homestead, a callout, the hogtied, a bet on a fight played one of the game's recorded remarks picked by its tone, which often didn't fit the words. Now those lines are said in the speaker's own voice, word for word, and the recorded remark is dropped when they are. At most one such line every five seconds, on top of the voice server's own queue, so the graphics card isn't crowded. Without the voice server, nothing changes. Not yet heard in game.

- **Worries you can see** (`[Mind] WorriesShown`, on, with `[Mind] MindLive` and `MindActs`; settings version 260; FIVEDAY item 7, from the plan Lex asked for on 2026-11-05: "worries are 42% of the thoughts over heads, but nothing shows them. People would act them out in small ways"). The one worry each townsperson carries this week now bends what they do, most at its worst and not at all once it's over: a man in debt keeps to his work and away from the card table, a parent whose child has the fever heads home at any hour and stops to pray, someone waiting on a letter goes by the store's counter again, a toothache sits down and reaches for the whiskey, a farmer in a dry spell keeps an eye on the sky, a man with a wanted poster on his mind keeps to the edges. When they act on it, the thought over their head says why ("Maybe today. The mail came in on the noon train."). Nothing new is asked of the game: every act is one the minds already play. Why it reads true: a worry narrows attention and bends the day round itself, so from the street it shows as a pattern of small choices. Offline, at 2 pm on a weekday the worry's own acts went from 10% to 35% of choices for a toothache and from 0% to 35% for a fever. Not yet seen in game.

- **The street's quarrels, debts, gossip and paper reading come many more ways** (FIVEDAY item 6; the 11-05 plan: "more variants for the scenes that repeat most ... the argument, gossip, the debt and the newspaper scene"). Twenty-four everyday quarrels instead of six (a ditch turned onto the wrong side, a saw never returned, hogs in the potatoes, a fiddle at midnight, a vote for a cousin, a claim stake kicked over), six ways to call in a debt and eight ways it ends (a deputy promised, the saddle taken, Mondays worked off, five a week shaken on), twelve ways to pass on gossip instead of four (the barber, the postmaster's wife, a cousin who swears to it), and six ways to read the paper aloud with seven answers to each kind of news. Measured with the ride harness over an hour of street scenes without the model: a line heard twice in the hour went from 18% to 11%; the argument's repeats from nine a run to two. Lines only, under each scene's own switch.

- **The quiet talks beside the street's scene actually start** (`[Scenes] SideTalkMetres`, 5.5; settings version 259; FIVEDAY item 5; SIXDAY's note to Lex: "the quiet talks of ScenesAtOnce never started in your 3.9 hours"). With `ScenesAtOnce` at 2 or more, two people near you may stop and talk while the main scene plays; until now they had to be standing still within 3.4 m of each other, which in a street of people walking somewhere almost never happened (Lex's log: 89 scenes, not one talk beside them). Now two within 5.5 m, standing or strolling, may stop where they are and talk, the way neighbours do who meet in the street; one new talk every 25 seconds at the most, so the street gets livelier without becoming a babble. Why it reads true: most of a town's talk is two people meeting by chance and stopping, not two who were already standing together. Offline, a look finds two who can stop and talk 9% of the time with six people near before, 55% now. Not yet seen in game.

- **Allies who remember** (`[Scenes] AlliesRemember`, on, with `[Scenes] FriendCalls`; settings version 258; FIVEDAY item 4, from the plan Lex asked for on 2026-11-05: "someone who fought beside you comes over later and mentions it, and mourns an ally who fell"). Someone who stood with you to the end of a fight, two game hours to a week later, the first time they're near and see you stopped, comes over to talk it over ("My hands shook for an hour after. Yours didn't. I noticed."), and if a comrade they knew fell in it, of him ("Lit a candle for Abner Sunday. Thought you'd want to know somebody did."). Once a fight; it's the first thing they bring most of the time. Why it reads true: people who have faced danger together seek each other out after, to make sense of it with the one other person who was there. Not yet seen in game.

- **A fight says more than the same few things** (FIVEDAY item 3; Lex, 2026-11-04: "focus on developing out what we do have much further"; brawls "seem good" in game). Every pool of a fight's words is three or four times deeper: the law breaking it up, letting a man off and taking one in, a fighter stopping, a brawler turning on you, the crowd. The brawl now opens a dozen ways instead of three ("That was my seat." "Didn't see you sitting in it."), and ends with eight winner's and eight loser's lines each. Bystanders now shout for the one they favour: a friend of one man, or an enemy of the other, cheers on his own man (before, a friend of one could be heard shouting for the other); the sharp jeer at both, the cold mutter, the kind cry for someone to stop it, and nobody cheers a fight with a woman in it. Offline: inside one fight a line came round again 29% of the time; now 9%. Lines only, under each feature's own switch.

- **After a fistfight, the beaten man remembers** (`[Scenes] Rematches`, on, with `[Mind] MindPlans`; settings version 257; FIVEDAY item 2, from the plan Lex asked for on 2026-11-05: "the loser remembers. Days later, when the two meet, there's a rematch, a sullen crossing of the street, or a handshake, depending on their natures"). The one left on the ground after a fight between two of the street's people takes it his own way, and half a game day or more later, when the two are near each other and near you, it plays out: a hothead or a proud man wants it again, with words of his own ("Got lucky the other day. Let's see you do it twice."), and it comes to blows; a fearful one, or one knocked cold, crosses the street to keep clear while the winner has his say; a kind or trusting one walks over and offers his hand, taken unless there was bad blood between them before the fight, when it's refused about half the time. Old friends mend it oftener; old enemies want it again oftener. Most let it lie. His thoughts carry it meanwhile ("Jaw's healing. Pride isn't."). Why it reads true: a public defeat costs standing, and each person repairs it the way their nature allows: the score evened, the danger avoided, or the tie mended. Offline: of 2,000 beaten men, 11% want a rematch, 13% keep clear, 10% offer a hand, 63% let it lie. Testing row "After a fight: a rematch, keeping clear, a handshake". Not yet seen in game.

- **Bets on a fistfight** (`[Scenes] BrawlWagers`, on; settings version 256; FIVEDAY item 1, from the plan Lex asked for on 2026-11-05: "onlookers bet among themselves on a fight. After it, money changes hands between them ... the loser's backer grumbles, or refuses to pay, which can start a new quarrel"). While two of the street's people trade blows near you, those watching who RealNPCs knows may put money on it: a friend of one of the fighters backs his friend, someone with bad blood against one backs the other, and the rest by their nature (the reckless and the drinking bet, the careful and the churchgoing rarely, the Devout never). Two who back different men and stand within a few steps of each other strike the bet aloud, two bits to five dollars, up to three bets a fight. When it's over the winner's backer crows, and the loser's pays, pays with a sour word, says he'll owe it till payday, or won't pay at all, by who he is: the honest and a friend always pay, a hothead in drink is the likeliest to welsh, and a lawman near makes him think twice. A welshed bet leaves bad blood between the two who made it, so the next time they meet it may come to words, and both remember it, as does anyone who saw. Nobody walks over or stands in a ring for it (that froze people before); Arthur is never part of it. Why it reads true: a stake turns a watcher into a partisan, and a man who backed the loser blames the fight before his own judgement. Offline: about one onlooker in six bets; a lost bet is paid half the time, paid sour a quarter, owed one in ten, welshed one in seven. Testing row "A fistfight with bets on it". Not yet seen in game.

- **Review fixes, Run 3 items 5-7** (an independent review). A friend no longer warns that "the law has been asking" after an ordinary visit to town or a purchase: only a known violence or theft of yours there in the last two days counts, as for the warnings. A friend sat, leaning or at work stays put, as the lonely and the favours do; one you aim at stops coming (no gift at gunpoint); a woman doesn't walk up after dark or indoors (the period's rule the late thanks keep); a friend with the gunsmith's favour and you with no gun may still come with something else; the Testing row says when FriendCalls is off. The friend's walk counts as a walk-up, so no other walk-up starts during it. Returners: once a town or a build has no outfit left, one of those already dressed comes back instead of every later body waiting on someone who can't be dressed. The quiet gate now sees a pause, a load, a fade and your death (the tick doesn't run then) and a fade coming back in, so no look is read for five seconds after any of them. One warning line no longer says "a man" for anyone; three greetings back that answered a question nobody asked are gone.

- **A translation file of your own** (`[Output] Translate`, on; settings version 255; Run 3, item 8; a player's ask relayed by Lex: "an external language file system (like English<TAB>translation per line) so it can be translated?"). Make `translation.txt` in the RealNPCs folder (beside the saves): one line each, the English, a TAB, your translation, saved as UTF-8; a line starting with # is a note, and a later line for the same English wins. The English may keep RealNPCs' {slots} ({who}, {town}, {a}, {b}...), and your translation puts them where your language wants them, so one entry covers every name. A line with no entry of its own is tried sentence by sentence; anything not found shows in English. It is read when the game starts and again within ten seconds of saving it. What it changes: the bar, the thoughts over heads, the subtitles and the hints, each line translated once as it first shows. What it doesn't: the cloned voices still say the English, the game's own recorded words stay the game's, the AI model's words are as it writes them, and the settings panel and the ini stay in English. The Testing row "Write the translation template" writes every line RealNPCs has (19,242 of them), one a line with a TAB after it, to translation-template.txt there, ready to fill in. Not yet seen in game; letters outside the Latin alphabet depend on the game's font.

- **Friends come to you** (`[Scenes] FriendCalls`, on; `FriendGifts`, on; settings version 254; Run 3, item 7; Lex's goal: "Friendly NPCs without Talk: friends come to you (warnings, news, small gifts) and favours by trade"). A friend of yours in town (the friend dot's rule: a full bar, a kindness of yours remembered, a trouble you mended, someone you saved) who sees you stopped now and then walks over unasked, as someone you helped comes to thank you, and brings one of four things. A warning: someone here mourns one you killed or is still hot with anger at you (named only if your friend would know them), or the law has been asking after a deed of yours in this town. The news: the year's freshest story, in their own words. A small gift: a few cents (a dollar from the well-off), a dozen cartridges for the gun you carry, or something to eat. Or a favour of their trade: the doctor looks you over and won't take a cent (your health back), the gunsmith tops up your rounds, the barkeep stands you a drink (your stamina), the store, the butcher, the hotel or a farm gives you something for the road (your health core). They say it to your face, hand it over, think a thought of their own and go on their way. About once in six game hours at most (some twelve real minutes), a friend once in two days; never in a mission, a scene, or while you're wanted, fighting or aiming. With `FriendGifts` off they bring words only. Nothing in Talk changes. The Testing row "A friend comes over (a warning, news, a gift, a favour)" brings the nearest friend at once (a test hands nothing over). Not yet seen in game.

- **2,482 more lines where people are already seen and heard** (Run 3, item 6; Lex, 2026-11-04: "focus on developing out what we do have much further such as thousands of thoughts, voice lines, unique interactions that are already proven"). Into the pools that show most and repeat most, measured first (a survey of all 15,864 lines in 4,140 pools, and Lex's own logs: worries 42% of the thoughts over people's heads, memories 28%, enRAGE's townsfolk 15%). The worries: ten more lines for every stage of all fourteen (700; sixteen a stage now, where there were six). Memories: every one of the 353 cells brought to six lines (778). Ties between people: the 35 pools of two or three lines brought to eight (201). Where people are: the counter keepers (8 more each), the places indoors (8, the saloon 12), the weather (6), each trade (4), each town (4), each temper and faith (5) (420). About you: the townsfolk who see you again, on horseback or with a gun out (15 each), and what enRAGE's townsfolk think of you by how they feel (2 to 4 lines a feeling became 12 to 14) (140). Said aloud: the street's kind, cold and sharp answers to trouble and news, the snaps, quarrels and making up, the debts (6 each; a debt's answer now comes from every line of its stage), the greetings in passing (5 each by the hour), and the talks' openers, needs, comfort on a worry and word of the stranger (243). In the offline three-hour ride (ridetest), lines already seen earlier in the session fell from 14% to 8% in the second hour and from 30% to 22% in the third; a quiet day's 880 thoughts hold 758 different lines, where they held 572. The new lines are under their features' own switches; all are original to RealNPCs (both originality scans unchanged).

- **Review fixes, Run 3 items 3-4** (an independent review). A death from a fall or in the river was written as "a gun, their own" in the log, the ledger and the history of one of enRAGE's people; now "stepping off a high edge" and "walking into the water". The drop to step off is found far more carefully: measured from the edge, not from where they stood, with both ground reads past it 7 m down; only over ground a person walks (a rise or dip of half a metre a step, where 1.2 m let steep slopes and fences through); never from indoors, never where the landing is water, never with the player below where they'd come down, and never with a rail, fence or wall knee-high on the walk. They count as over the edge only once past it (walking uphill to the edge, or through a dip, could count as a fall at the first step); held at the edge, they go limp and are pushed once the body is loose, and if they stay on the edge the log says so. The drop is looked for only when it could be chosen. By a campfire, the others' answers follow the tale actually told when NightTales is switched mid-fire. The tales: the Cajun charm against the feu follet is a needle in a fence post; the Lost Dutchman's woman hunted one summer and then sold maps; a scoff at a tall tale isn't about ghost stories (nor "grown men" when a woman tells it); "that's on you" and "never tell it again" reworded, and the teller is no longer called Clem, the name of one of the listeners. The settings and the readme no longer say the fall isn't built.

- **Returning people dressed as themselves, and a careful probe of people's looks** (`[World] Returners`, off as it comes, `ReturnersChance` 35, `ReturnersPerTown` 12; `LooksReadDepth` 1; settings version 253; Run 3, item 5: the recognition plan's stages 1 and 2, with the guards of its stage 0; Lex, 2026-11-04: "Yes start recognition plan"). The game keeps nobody's identity: a passer-by met again is a new body, its clothes rolled anew. With Returners on, someone who matters to RealNPCs (woken, met before, a worry at its worst) comes back unseen, 20 to 150 metres from you, in a body of their own build that the town regulars' scan has found, dressed in an outfit of their own. The first time they come back they're given one that none of the town's regulars, other returners or posts wears in that build, and from then on they look the same each time; their very first body keeps the game's clothes, since no native says which outfit the game rolled. Nothing is read off the body: the identity is written onto it. With it on, the woken no longer turn up in a stranger's body in plain sight. It is off as it comes because enRAGE may take a dressed body for one of its own people: the log now says each time it does (`CAST taken up by enRAGE`, for the town regulars and the test cast too). The Testing row "Read the looks of the people near you" (`TestLooksProbeKey`) is the only read of anyone's clothes there is (the clothes reads stay held back since the Saint Denis freezes): one press reads up to six people within 40 m at `LooksReadDepth` (1 counts the pieces, 2 reads them, 3 their tints, 4 their kinds), one tier of the game's calls at a time, each read timed and set beside enRAGE's own print of the body. Its guards: no look is read while the world is quiet (a load, a fade, a death, an arrest, a story mission, the controls taken, a pause) or for 5 to 10 seconds after; never a body dressed in the last 10 seconds (every place RealNPCs dresses a body now notes it, and the waking's strip no longer reads the body it has just undressed); the hang watch names the call and the piece it's in; and `lastread.txt` in the RealNPCs folder says which body, piece and call is being read, so a freeze leaves the call that held it. The store of people is kept over its cap, rather than failing, when nobody may be dropped. F7 adds `TEST returners:` and `TEST looks probe:`. Not built: knowing people again by their look (stage 3, which waits on the probe's tiers passing in game), holding a few people near (the plan keeps it off until tested) and a returner's own voice.

- **Despair: measured, a fall from a height, and the waking tuned up** (`[WestWorld] DespairFall`, on; `AwakeningShare` 3, `AwakeningDays` 3, `AwakeningDespair` 65; settings version 252; Run 3, item 4; Lex, 2026-11-04: "I haven't seen many despair ending recently, we can tune up the amount of awake people, and frequency"). Measured first: today's log had no despair ending in about 2.4 hours with West World on (one stage advance in 3.9 hours; the ini has had it off since), and Lex's ini holds the waking far below the defaults (AwakeningShare 0.45, AwakeningDays 6). Offline, the Engine's own waking over 40 hours of play (mindtest `DespairRateTest`: 60 people met an hour, half the awake met again): at Lex's values one despair ending every 80 hours; at 0.7.24's defaults one every 4.4 hours, the first awake after 14 hours; now one every 1.9 hours, the first awake after 7. The fall: with a sheer drop of 7 m or more near (the game's own ground height read in sixteen directions: walking ground to an edge, then a step down of 3.5 m or more), half of those who would run at a wall or take a knife walk to the edge instead, turn and look out, and step off; if the game holds them at the edge they're tipped over it. The gun, the poison and the river keep their own chances. An awake person's act comes every 75 seconds, not 120. The Testing row "Despair now" goes through the river and the fall too (a fall with no drop near falls back to the wall, the river or the knife; the river to the wall or the knife). Not yet seen in game.
- **The old tales, told at night** (`[Scenes] NightTales`, on; settings version 251; Run 3, item 3; Lex, 2026-11-04, on the run's question 10: "this is good with people telling night folklore"). After dark, in the saloon (most often) and in the street, a street scene may be one of thirteen old tales of the country told line by line (`Core/Folklore.cs`): the bell on the grave, the tommyknockers, the feu follet, the Bell Witch, the phantom stage, the Lost Dutchman's mine, Tailypo, the fiddler at the crossroads, La Llorona, the brakeman's lantern, John Henry, the hoop snake, the white pacing stallion. The listener answers by their nature (a coward shivers at a ghost, the hard scoff, the rest lean in) and has the last word. By a campfire in the wilds the man who calls you over tells one of them about half the time, the others at the fire answering it. Testing row "An old tale told after dark"; the log's `SCENE ... nighttale`.
- **People talk over the times** (`[Mind] TimesTalk`, on; `TimesTalkShare` 100; settings version 250; Run 3, item 2; Lex, 2026-11-04: "Ideally the player will just hear lots of talk relating to the time of living in America midwest 1899", who holds office among it, through the talks between people, not Talk). Any two who stop near you, friends or strangers, may talk over the news of the day: an almanac story of the fortnight, or a topic of the year. Fourteen new topics, 165 lines: who holds office (McKinley and Mark Hanna; Vice President Hobart to October (he died on 21 November; the almanac has it), then nobody in the chair; Root at the War Department after Alger from August; Henderson of Iowa in Reed's chair from December; Roosevelt, Fairbanks, Taft, Root and Uncle Joe Cannon in 1907; these count double), and how the heartland lives (the wheat and the bank, the railroad's rates, the catalogue and the mail route, the dry vote, the old soldiers and their pensions, the telephone, the bicycle, the camp meeting). Measured offline on the same street (mindtest `TimesTalkRateTest`): talk of the times went from one main scene in fifty (about half a talk an hour; Lex's logs had 1.3 an hour) to one in fourteen, about 1.6 an hour at 23 main scenes an hour, and to more than a quarter of the second scenes that play beside them (none before); thefts, begging and people's own news exactly as before (it stands below them: an independent review found the first tuning took a quarter of them). On the topics that have them, a talk of the times sometimes goes a second round with the first speaker's follow-up and another answer, never the same answer twice. By day in the street, any hour in the saloon; said in their own voices with the voice server. Off with `[World] HistoricalAccuracy`. Testing row "Two people talk over the times"; F7 `TEST mind talks:` counts them.
- **enRAGE's people go by the name enRAGE shows** (Run 3, item 1; Lex's /goal: "enRAGE's visible name (its card and prompt) everywhere, its acquaintances included, and nothing of ours written into enRAGE's records"). Lex's first session with 0.7.24's fixes logged one man as Friedrich Keller, then Walter Odell, then Tip Pierce within 22 seconds: enRAGE's card and lock-on prompt read one list of names, its saved record of the person another, and RealNPCs used to write its own names into those records when enRAGE took someone up, which a reload undid on the card but not in the record. Now every name RealNPCs shows or remembers for one of enRAGE's people (the subtitles, the Nearby line, the minds, the name cards, the greeting's note, the witness and host lines) is the one enRAGE's own card shows, and RealNPCs writes no names into enRAGE's records at all. Its own people keep their names: a town regular, cast into a new body each time, and the test cast would otherwise take whatever name enRAGE gave each new body by its look (0.7.24 renamed them so); for them, and for a relative made for the part, RealNPCs sets enRAGE's lock-on label for that body, for the session only, so the prompt says who they are.

## 0.7.24 (2026-11-04)

- **Brawls escalate more easily, and people may flee from them** (`[World] Bravery`; settings version 248; Lex, 2026-11-04: "if we can have the brawls escalate easier but if some NPCs want to flee, that is okay"). Bravery 85, where it comes, now adds a tenth to every onlooker's pull to join a fistfight (in the offline test about three and a half times as many strangers step in); 50 is how they joined before; 0, only a fighter's friend or foe. The fighters no longer carry the combat setting that stopped anyone fleeing from them, which left frightened onlookers cowering where they stood; they keep the one the game's own street fights use, so a fistfight still doesn't empty the street.
- **Fewer hitches: the save every ten minutes, and enRAGE's people read a few at a time** (Lex, 2026-11-04: "Yes we can save less often, we could even do every 10 minutes"). The memory of people was saved once a minute in play, a 22-26 ms frame each time; now every ten minutes in play, and still within 20 s at any fade, load or fast travel, so a crash in play can lose up to ten minutes of what people remember. Reading enRAGE's people every five seconds took 9-12 ms in one frame; now about 1.5 ms a frame over several frames, and the add-on sees them all together as before (log `OBSERVE enRAGE's people read a slice a frame: N people over M frames`).
- **One name for one body** (Lex, 2026-11-04: a brawl and the Nearby line said Malachi and Tucker, the name cards Per and Tucker; "Let's go with your suggestion for matching the names"). enRAGE names the people near you for its lock-on prompt before it takes anyone up; the scenes used that name, the name card and the minds RealNPCs' own. Now RealNPCs' record for the body takes enRAGE's name the moment anything looks the person up, everywhere it keeps it, someone you'd already met under RealNPCs' name included (Lex: "we should try to have enrage name win there"). enRAGE names everyone within 40 m by their look about once a second and gives a face it has seen before the same name, so RealNPCs' name gives way before you meet most people, and a name you've learned changes once at most (log `NAME ... goes by ..., enRAGE's name for the body, everywhere`, with "you'd met them as ..." when you had).
- **A relative who walks up is the game's again once they've spoken** (Lex, 2026-11-04: no lock-on for several seconds on someone who walked up and asked after him; "hand them back to the game after they complete what they were made for"). Someone made for the part was held until eight seconds after speaking, and the game offers no lock-on for a body a script holds; now they're handed back 2.5 s after they start, so you can lock on and answer while they're still talking. Their name goes into enRAGE's prompt list too, so the prompt says the same name. The friend riding in already goes back to the game when the fight is over; stalkers and the encounters' people keep to their scenes. Everyone RealNPCs made and hands back alive can now be greeted, antagonized and robbed like anyone the game made (Lex, testing it: "I can now lock on to the person walking up but no option to greet them"): a ped a script makes has no interaction personality, so the game offered no Greet; the hand-back now copies one from a townsperson about and sets the game's lock-on interaction flags, as enRAGE does for its own made people (log `CAST ... is the game's again ... s after speaking, its lock-on prompts and all`).
- **The AI model: smaller as it comes, and off the card sooner** (`[Mind] LanguageModel`; settings version 249; Lex, 2026-11-04, "Yes for now"). `llama3.2:3b`, about 2.3 GB of the graphics card, in place of `llama3.1:8b`, about 5 GB; offline it passed 98 of 99 thoughts and 50 of 60 Talk answers through the checks, about a tenth of a second each (an answer that fails a check gives way to the written line, as always). An ini holding the stock 8B moves to the 3B, and until you run `ollama pull llama3.2:3b` the 8B goes on being used, so nobody's AI goes quiet; a model of your own is kept. Ollama is asked to give the card back after five minutes with nothing asked (thirty before).
- **The voice pack stays on a big card** (`voice/voice_server.py`; Lex, 2026-11-04). On a graphics card of 12 GB or more the voice model stays on the card while you play, so a line after a quiet spell never waits for it to come back (moving 2-3 GB back onto a nearly full card was a hitch of its own); on a smaller card it leaves after a quiet minute, as before. `--idle-seconds` still decides when it's given.
- **Roosevelt and the telephone in 1899** (Lex, 2026-11-04: "let them in, let all of these real 1899 thoughts in"). Both were real in 1899: Roosevelt the Rough Rider and governor of New York, the telephone in the big towns and spreading along the farm roads. People may think of them now: a new everyday talking point (the telephone spreading) and Roosevelt by name in the almanac's thoughts. The harnesses' rule that kept both to 1907 is gone.
- **Not changed: Talk's Goodbye.** The half minute the Talk list stayed up after Goodbye on 2026-11-04 was the game itself not running (both the list's close and the answer's subtitle wait on the game's clock, which had stopped), with the 8B model and the voice pack crowding a nearly full card; the smaller model and the voice pack's change are the fix for that. The camp prompts after it are still unexplained.

## 0.7.23 (2026-11-04)

- **Bravery is only about brawls now** (`[World] Bravery`; settings version 247; Lex, 2026-11-04: "Can we have it so bravery only effects their chance to join a brawl - and not the flee or cower flag?"). Bravery no longer sets the game's cower-instead-of-flee flag on anyone, which is what left townsfolk frozen in place after a killing (the Valentine saloon, then Strawberry), and `BraveStand`, meant to stand them up, is gone with it: it never once fired, since it refused anyone the game counts as fleeing, and a cowering person is. Instead Bravery pulls on an onlooker's choice to join a fistfight: 85, where it comes, joins exactly as before; higher, more step in (at 100 several times as many); lower, fewer; at 0 only a fighter's friend or foe. The game's own fleeing and cowering are the game's again. Two things stay: a fistfight still doesn't send the crowd running (the game's own street-fight setting on the fighters, now at any Bravery), and the mod's own frights still let brave onlookers stand back and watch instead of being sent running.

## 0.7.22 (2026-11-04)

- **The almanac: the year's real news in people's heads and mouths** (`[World] Almanac`, on, `AlmanacShare` 100; settings version 246; Lex, 2026-11-04: "I haven't noticed much historical accuracy voicelines, inner thoughts or subtitles"). 285 real events and talking points: 206 of 1899 (130 dated, from the flag going up over Havana on New Year's Day through the war at Manila, the great cold of February, the Bunker Hill mill blown up, Pearl Hart and the Globe stage, the Overland Flyer robbed at Wilcox, Jeffries, the Brazos flood, Black Jack Ketchum, Nome's beach gold, Dewey's homecoming, the Boer war, Hobart's death, Lawton and Moody in December; and 76 of the year's everyday talk: prices and wages, the trusts, the Sears book, the wheel, the horseless carriage, ragtime and Hello Ma Baby, the patent medicines, where Frank James and Wyatt Earp and Geronimo got to) and 79 of 1907 for the epilogue (the Thaw trial, the midnight forests, Haywood at Boise, the Panic, Oklahoma's statehood, Monongah, the white fleet; the nickelodeon, the party line, the grazing fee). Each is known from the day it could be in a Western paper (news from Samoa and Hawaii came by ship), the everyday talk never before its time (a song or a book not out in January), is news while it's fresh (a month to three by its size), and has a headline, a paper sentence and two to four lines of what one person thinks or says of it, each naming what it's about.
